========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do, though I feel a bit embarrassed saying it.

Before work, or if we’re going to a family event, I can get stuck in the bathroom for a long time checking the mirror. Sometimes it’s not just once or twice… it can turn into hours, taking pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to see if my mouth looks uneven, fixing my makeup, adjusting a scarf, then checking again.

I’ll ask my husband, “Does my face look lopsided?” probably more than is fair to him. And sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women, comparing my face to theirs, which I know isn’t helpful. I don’t want to sound vain or silly, but it’s hard to stop once I start.

I don’t want to sound vain or silly, but it’s hard to stop once I start.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, like checking mirrors or asking your husband, or can you stop yourself when you want to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / architect / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors\nmanifestation: \"Before work or any family event I can spend hours checking the bathroom mirror, taking phone pictures from different angles, redoing my makeup and scarf, asking my husband if my face looks lopsided, and then scrolling through other women’s photos to see how bad mine looks beside theirs.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I want to say I can stop, because I know how it sounds… but honestly, once I’ve started, it feels really hard to pull away.

Before work or a family event, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check quickly, and then I’m still in the bathroom ages later, looking in the mirror, taking pictures from different angles, redoing my makeup or my scarf, and checking again. I’ll ask my husband if my face looks lopsided even when I know I’ve already asked.

And then sometimes I end up looking at other women’s photos, comparing, just to see how bad mine looks next to theirs. I don’t feel proud of it. It’s not like I want to spend hours doing that… it just feels like I have to know before I can go out. Does that make sense?

I’d like to think I could change my mind about it… I really would. I don’t want to be someone who’s just stubborn about this.

But when I see my mouth or my jaw in a photo, or even just catch it in a reflection, the droop and the unevenness feel so obvious to me. Even if my husband or other people say I look fine, part of me thinks they’re just being kind, or they don’t see it the way I do.

So… I suppose my view feels pretty fixed right now. I’m here because I can’t seem to stop seeing it, and it’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations or seeing clients because I feel like everyone will notice. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?

I think… it’s mostly about how I look, or how I imagine I look to other people. But it also feels like something is just wrong when I see it, if that makes sense.

I’m here because I can’t seem to stop noticing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. In photos or reflections, it just jumps out at me. Even when people say I look fine, I feel like it’s obvious, and then I worry others must see it too.

I know that may sound a bit excessive, but it’s gotten to where I’ve avoided presentations and meeting clients because I’m afraid they’ll be looking at that instead of listening to me. Is that the sort of distinction you mean?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you for seeing me. I’m probably making a bigger deal of it than I should, but I’ve been getting really stuck on how my mouth and jaw look. There’s this droop, or unevenness, that I see in photos and reflections, and I can’t seem to stop noticing it.\n\nOther people tell me I look fine, so I know this may sound a bit silly, but to me it feels very obvious. It’s gotten to the point where I’ve started avoiding presentations and meeting with clients, which is… not great for my work.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your mouth and jaw that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you also worry about?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s my mouth and jaw. I mean, I notice other things like anyone does, but this is the thing I get stuck on—the droop, the unevenness around my mouth. I see it in photos or even just reflections, and then it’s hard to unsee it.\n\nI know people say I look fine, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it feels really obvious to me… enough that I’ve been avoiding presentations and meeting clients. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about your mouth and jaw is causing you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty distressing, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s there most days, maybe every day. I’ll catch it in a photo or a reflection and then I can’t stop seeing the droop or unevenness around my mouth and jaw.\n\nEven when people tell me I look fine, it still feels really obvious to me. It’s upsetting enough that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients, which I know isn’t good. I’m not sure if that answers it the right way, but it feels like it’s taking up a lot of space in my day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family due to your concerns about your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit. I’ve been trying to keep it from affecting people around me, but it sort of is.\n\nI cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I just couldn’t face being seen like that, and I’ve been skipping some networking things connected to an award, which sounds awful when I say it out loud. Then deadlines start slipping, and at home I’ve missed or backed out of family gatherings too.\n\nI don’t mean to be difficult or dramatic. It’s just… everything feels like it’s starting to fall apart around this one thing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of your concerns about your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I feel a bit embarrassed saying it.\n\nBefore work, or if we’re going to a family event, I can get stuck in the bathroom for a long time checking the mirror. Sometimes it’s not just once or twice… it can turn into hours, taking pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to see if my mouth looks uneven, fixing my makeup, adjusting a scarf, then checking again.\n\nI’ll ask my husband, “Does my face look lopsided?” probably more than is fair to him. And sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women, comparing my face to theirs, which I know isn’t helpful. I don’t want to sound vain or silly, but it’s hard to stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, like checking mirrors or asking your husband, or can you stop yourself when you want to?\n\nPatient: I want to say I can stop, because I know how it sounds… but honestly, once I’ve started, it feels really hard to pull away.\n\nBefore work or a family event, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check quickly, and then I’m still in the bathroom ages later, looking in the mirror, taking pictures from different angles, redoing my makeup or my scarf, and checking again. I’ll ask my husband if my face looks lopsided even when I know I’ve already asked.\n\nAnd then sometimes I end up looking at other women’s photos, comparing, just to see how bad mine looks next to theirs. I don’t feel proud of it. It’s not like I want to spend hours doing that… it just feels like I have to know before I can go out. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think it would be possible for you to change your opinion about the appearance of your mouth and jaw, or do you feel like your view of it is fixed?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I could change my mind about it… I really would. I don’t want to be someone who’s just stubborn about this.\n\nBut when I see my mouth or my jaw in a photo, or even just catch it in a reflection, the droop and the unevenness feel so obvious to me. Even if my husband or other people say I look fine, part of me thinks they’re just being kind, or they don’t see it the way I do.\n\nSo… I suppose my view feels pretty fixed right now. I’m here because I can’t seem to stop seeing it, and it’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations or seeing clients because I feel like everyone will notice. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your mouth and jaw are more about how you look to others or about something feeling 'not right' to you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly about how I look, or how I imagine I look to other people. But it also feels like something is just wrong when I see it, if that makes sense.\n\nI’m here because I can’t seem to stop noticing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. In photos or reflections, it just jumps out at me. Even when people say I look fine, I feel like it’s obvious, and then I worry others must see it too.\n\nI know that may sound a bit excessive, but it’s gotten to where I’ve avoided presentations and meeting clients because I’m afraid they’ll be looking at that instead of listening to me. Is that the sort of distinction you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It feels… more automatic than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself I’m just going to check once and then get on with things, but before work or if we’re going to see family, it can turn into hours in the bathroom.

I’ll check the mirror, then take pictures on my phone from different angles, fix my makeup or my scarf, ask my husband if my face looks lopsided… and sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women to compare. I know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not doing it because I enjoy it. It’s like once the thought is there, I have to keep checking until I feel sure, and I don’t really feel sure for long.

It’s just… everything feels like it’s starting to fall apart around this one thing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of your concerns about your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I feel a bit embarrassed saying it.\n\nBefore work, or if we’re going to a family event, I can get stuck in the bathroom for a long time checking the mirror. Sometimes it’s not just once or twice… it can turn into hours, taking pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to see if my mouth looks uneven, fixing my makeup, adjusting a scarf, then checking again.\n\nI’ll ask my husband, “Does my face look lopsided?” probably more than is fair to him. And sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women, comparing my face to theirs, which I know isn’t helpful. I don’t want to sound vain or silly, but it’s hard to stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, like checking mirrors or asking your husband, or can you stop yourself when you want to?\n\nPatient: I want to say I can stop, because I know how it sounds… but honestly, once I’ve started, it feels really hard to pull away.\n\nBefore work or a family event, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check quickly, and then I’m still in the bathroom ages later, looking in the mirror, taking pictures from different angles, redoing my makeup or my scarf, and checking again. I’ll ask my husband if my face looks lopsided even when I know I’ve already asked.\n\nAnd then sometimes I end up looking at other women’s photos, comparing, just to see how bad mine looks next to theirs. I don’t feel proud of it. It’s not like I want to spend hours doing that… it just feels like I have to know before I can go out. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think it would be possible for you to change your opinion about the appearance of your mouth and jaw, or do you feel like your view of it is fixed?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I could change my mind about it… I really would. I don’t want to be someone who’s just stubborn about this.\n\nBut when I see my mouth or my jaw in a photo, or even just catch it in a reflection, the droop and the unevenness feel so obvious to me. Even if my husband or other people say I look fine, part of me thinks they’re just being kind, or they don’t see it the way I do.\n\nSo… I suppose my view feels pretty fixed right now. I’m here because I can’t seem to stop seeing it, and it’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations or seeing clients because I feel like everyone will notice. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your mouth and jaw are more about how you look to others or about something feeling 'not right' to you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly about how I look, or how I imagine I look to other people. But it also feels like something is just wrong when I see it, if that makes sense.\n\nI’m here because I can’t seem to stop noticing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. In photos or reflections, it just jumps out at me. Even when people say I look fine, I feel like it’s obvious, and then I worry others must see it too.\n\nI know that may sound a bit excessive, but it’s gotten to where I’ve avoided presentations and meeting clients because I’m afraid they’ll be looking at that instead of listening to me. Is that the sort of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance or thinking about your mouth and jaw is something you can control, or does it feel like it's happening automatically?\n\nPatient: It feels… more automatic than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself I’m just going to check once and then get on with things, but before work or if we’re going to see family, it can turn into hours in the bathroom.\n\nI’ll check the mirror, then take pictures on my phone from different angles, fix my makeup or my scarf, ask my husband if my face looks lopsided… and sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women to compare. I know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not doing it because I enjoy it. It’s like once the thought is there, I have to keep checking until I feel sure, and I don’t really feel sure for long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t want to sound vain or silly, but it’s hard to stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, like checking mirrors or asking your husband, or can you stop yourself when you want to?\n\nPatient: I want to say I can stop, because I know how it sounds… but honestly, once I’ve started, it feels really hard to pull away.\n\nBefore work or a family event, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check quickly, and then I’m still in the bathroom ages later, looking in the mirror, taking pictures from different angles, redoing my makeup or my scarf, and checking again. I’ll ask my husband if my face looks lopsided even when I know I’ve already asked.\n\nAnd then sometimes I end up looking at other women’s photos, comparing, just to see how bad mine looks next to theirs. I don’t feel proud of it. It’s not like I want to spend hours doing that… it just feels like I have to know before I can go out. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think it would be possible for you to change your opinion about the appearance of your mouth and jaw, or do you feel like your view of it is fixed?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I could change my mind about it… I really would. I don’t want to be someone who’s just stubborn about this.\n\nBut when I see my mouth or my jaw in a photo, or even just catch it in a reflection, the droop and the unevenness feel so obvious to me. Even if my husband or other people say I look fine, part of me thinks they’re just being kind, or they don’t see it the way I do.\n\nSo… I suppose my view feels pretty fixed right now. I’m here because I can’t seem to stop seeing it, and it’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations or seeing clients because I feel like everyone will notice. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your mouth and jaw are more about how you look to others or about something feeling 'not right' to you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly about how I look, or how I imagine I look to other people. But it also feels like something is just wrong when I see it, if that makes sense.\n\nI’m here because I can’t seem to stop noticing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. In photos or reflections, it just jumps out at me. Even when people say I look fine, I feel like it’s obvious, and then I worry others must see it too.\n\nI know that may sound a bit excessive, but it’s gotten to where I’ve avoided presentations and meeting clients because I’m afraid they’ll be looking at that instead of listening to me. Is that the sort of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance or thinking about your mouth and jaw is something you can control, or does it feel like it's happening automatically?\n\nPatient: It feels… more automatic than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself I’m just going to check once and then get on with things, but before work or if we’re going to see family, it can turn into hours in the bathroom.\n\nI’ll check the mirror, then take pictures on my phone from different angles, fix my makeup or my scarf, ask my husband if my face looks lopsided… and sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women to compare. I know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not doing it because I enjoy it. It’s like once the thought is there, I have to keep checking until I feel sure, and I don’t really feel sure for long.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific times of the day when your concerns about your mouth and jaw interfere with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… especially in the mornings, before I have to go to work or see people. I can lose so much time trying to make myself look acceptable that the day starts badly before I’ve even left the house.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it has interfered more than I’d like. I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I just couldn’t face standing there with everyone looking at me. And there have been some award-related networking events I should have gone to, but I’ve missed them because I couldn’t bear being seen.\n\nIt’s starting to affect project deadlines, and even family gatherings have become difficult. I know that’s not good… I’m trying not to let it run everything, but it feels like things are kind of falling apart around this. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I think it’s mostly the appearance itself… the droop, the unevenness around my mouth and jaw. I can’t seem to not see it in photos or reflections.

But then almost immediately I start thinking, well, if it looks that obvious to me, other people must notice it too. Even when someone tells me I look fine, I don’t really believe it for long. I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because I worry they’ll be looking at that instead of what I’m saying.

I know that may sound a bit much, but… that’s honestly how it feels.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you for seeing me. I’m probably making a bigger deal of it than I should, but I’ve been getting really stuck on how my mouth and jaw look. It’s gotten to the point where I’ve started avoiding presentations and meeting with clients, which is… not great for my work.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your mouth and jaw that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you also worry about?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s my mouth and jaw. Sometimes it’s not just once or twice… it can turn into hours, taking pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to see if my mouth looks uneven, fixing my makeup, adjusting a scarf, then checking again.\n\nI’ll ask my husband, “Does my face look lopsided?” probably more than is fair to him. And sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women, comparing my face to theirs, which I know isn’t helpful. I don’t want to sound vain or silly, but it’s hard to stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, like checking mirrors or asking your husband, or can you stop yourself when you want to?\n\nPatient: I want to say I can stop, because I know how it sounds… but honestly, once I’ve started, it feels really hard to pull away.\n\nBefore work or a family event, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check quickly, and then I’m still in the bathroom ages later, looking in the mirror, taking pictures from different angles, redoing my makeup or my scarf, and checking again. I’ll ask my husband if my face looks lopsided even when I know I’ve already asked.\n\nAnd then sometimes I end up looking at other women’s photos, comparing, just to see how bad mine looks next to theirs. I don’t feel proud of it. It’s not like I want to spend hours doing that… it just feels like I have to know before I can go out. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think it would be possible for you to change your opinion about the appearance of your mouth and jaw, or do you feel like your view of it is fixed?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I could change my mind about it… I really would. I don’t want to be someone who’s just stubborn about this.\n\nBut when I see my mouth or my jaw in a photo, or even just catch it in a reflection, the droop and the unevenness feel so obvious to me. Even if my husband or other people say I look fine, part of me thinks they’re just being kind, or they don’t see it the way I do.\n\nSo… I suppose my view feels pretty fixed right now. I’m here because I can’t seem to stop seeing it, and it’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations or seeing clients because I feel like everyone will notice. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your mouth and jaw are more about how you look to others or about something feeling 'not right' to you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly about how I look, or how I imagine I look to other people. But it also feels like something is just wrong when I see it, if that makes sense.\n\nI’m here because I can’t seem to stop noticing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. In photos or reflections, it just jumps out at me. Even when people say I look fine, I feel like it’s obvious, and then I worry others must see it too.\n\nI know that may sound a bit excessive, but it’s gotten to where I’ve avoided presentations and meeting clients because I’m afraid they’ll be looking at that instead of listening to me. Is that the sort of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance or thinking about your mouth and jaw is something you can control, or does it feel like it's happening automatically?\n\nPatient: It feels… more automatic than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself I’m just going to check once and then get on with things, but before work or if we’re going to see family, it can turn into hours in the bathroom.\n\nI’ll check the mirror, then take pictures on my phone from different angles, fix my makeup or my scarf, ask my husband if my face looks lopsided… and sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women to compare. I know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not doing it because I enjoy it. It’s like once the thought is there, I have to keep checking until I feel sure, and I don’t really feel sure for long.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific times of the day when your concerns about your mouth and jaw interfere with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… especially in the mornings, before I have to go to work or see people. I can lose so much time trying to make myself look acceptable that the day starts badly before I’ve even left the house.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it has interfered more than I’d like. I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I just couldn’t face standing there with everyone looking at me. And there have been some award-related networking events I should have gone to, but I’ve missed them because I couldn’t bear being seen.\n\nIt’s starting to affect project deadlines, and even family gatherings have become difficult. I know that’s not good… I’m trying not to let it run everything, but it feels like things are kind of falling apart around this. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you think about your mouth and jaw, are you more worried about the actual appearance or what others might think of it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the appearance itself… the droop, the unevenness around my mouth and jaw. I can’t seem to not see it in photos or reflections.\n\nBut then almost immediately I start thinking, well, if it looks that obvious to me, other people must notice it too. Even when someone tells me I look fine, I don’t really believe it for long. I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because I worry they’ll be looking at that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nI know that may sound a bit much, but… that’s honestly how it feels.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I think… it starts with the flaw itself, if that makes sense. I can’t stop seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw, especially in photos or reflections.

I’ll catch it in a photo or a reflection and then I can’t stop seeing the droop or unevenness around my mouth and jaw.\n\nEven when people tell me I look fine, it still feels really obvious to me. It’s upsetting enough that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients, which I know isn’t good. I’m not sure if that answers it the right way, but it feels like it’s taking up a lot of space in my day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family due to your concerns about your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit. I’ve been trying to keep it from affecting people around me, but it sort of is.\n\nI cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I just couldn’t face being seen like that, and I’ve been skipping some networking things connected to an award, which sounds awful when I say it out loud. Then deadlines start slipping, and at home I’ve missed or backed out of family gatherings too.\n\nI don’t mean to be difficult or dramatic. It’s just… everything feels like it’s starting to fall apart around this one thing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of your concerns about your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I feel a bit embarrassed saying it.\n\nBefore work, or if we’re going to a family event, I can get stuck in the bathroom for a long time checking the mirror. Sometimes it’s not just once or twice… it can turn into hours, taking pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to see if my mouth looks uneven, fixing my makeup, adjusting a scarf, then checking again.\n\nI’ll ask my husband, “Does my face look lopsided?” probably more than is fair to him. And sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women, comparing my face to theirs, which I know isn’t helpful. I don’t want to sound vain or silly, but it’s hard to stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, like checking mirrors or asking your husband, or can you stop yourself when you want to?\n\nPatient: I want to say I can stop, because I know how it sounds… but honestly, once I’ve started, it feels really hard to pull away.\n\nBefore work or a family event, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check quickly, and then I’m still in the bathroom ages later, looking in the mirror, taking pictures from different angles, redoing my makeup or my scarf, and checking again. I’ll ask my husband if my face looks lopsided even when I know I’ve already asked.\n\nAnd then sometimes I end up looking at other women’s photos, comparing, just to see how bad mine looks next to theirs. I don’t feel proud of it. It’s not like I want to spend hours doing that… it just feels like I have to know before I can go out. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think it would be possible for you to change your opinion about the appearance of your mouth and jaw, or do you feel like your view of it is fixed?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I could change my mind about it… I really would. I don’t want to be someone who’s just stubborn about this.\n\nBut when I see my mouth or my jaw in a photo, or even just catch it in a reflection, the droop and the unevenness feel so obvious to me. Even if my husband or other people say I look fine, part of me thinks they’re just being kind, or they don’t see it the way I do.\n\nSo… I suppose my view feels pretty fixed right now. I’m here because I can’t seem to stop seeing it, and it’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations or seeing clients because I feel like everyone will notice. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your mouth and jaw are more about how you look to others or about something feeling 'not right' to you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly about how I look, or how I imagine I look to other people. But it also feels like something is just wrong when I see it, if that makes sense.\n\nI’m here because I can’t seem to stop noticing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. In photos or reflections, it just jumps out at me. Even when people say I look fine, I feel like it’s obvious, and then I worry others must see it too.\n\nI know that may sound a bit excessive, but it’s gotten to where I’ve avoided presentations and meeting clients because I’m afraid they’ll be looking at that instead of listening to me. Is that the sort of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance or thinking about your mouth and jaw is something you can control, or does it feel like it's happening automatically?\n\nPatient: It feels… more automatic than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself I’m just going to check once and then get on with things, but before work or if we’re going to see family, it can turn into hours in the bathroom.\n\nI’ll check the mirror, then take pictures on my phone from different angles, fix my makeup or my scarf, ask my husband if my face looks lopsided… and sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women to compare. I know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not doing it because I enjoy it. It’s like once the thought is there, I have to keep checking until I feel sure, and I don’t really feel sure for long.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific times of the day when your concerns about your mouth and jaw interfere with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… especially in the mornings, before I have to go to work or see people. I can lose so much time trying to make myself look acceptable that the day starts badly before I’ve even left the house.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it has interfered more than I’d like. I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I just couldn’t face standing there with everyone looking at me. And there have been some award-related networking events I should have gone to, but I’ve missed them because I couldn’t bear being seen.\n\nIt’s starting to affect project deadlines, and even family gatherings have become difficult. I know that’s not good… I’m trying not to let it run everything, but it feels like things are kind of falling apart around this. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you think about your mouth and jaw, are you more worried about the actual appearance or what others might think of it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the appearance itself… the droop, the unevenness around my mouth and jaw. I can’t seem to not see it in photos or reflections.\n\nBut then almost immediately I start thinking, well, if it looks that obvious to me, other people must notice it too. Even when someone tells me I look fine, I don’t really believe it for long. I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because I worry they’ll be looking at that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nI know that may sound a bit much, but… that’s honestly how it feels.\n\nDoctor: When you worry about your mouth and jaw, is it more about the flaw itself or the fear of what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: I think… it starts with the flaw itself, if that makes sense. I can’t stop seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw, especially in photos or reflections.\n\nAnd then I suppose it turns into worrying that other people must see it too. Even when they tell me I look fine, I don’t really believe it for long. I know that may sound vain or silly, but it feels so obvious to me that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because of it. Is that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t want to sound vain or silly, but it’s hard to stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, like checking mirrors or asking your husband, or can you stop yourself when you want to?\n\nPatient: I want to say I can stop, because I know how it sounds… but honestly, once I’ve started, it feels really hard to pull away.\n\nBefore work or a family event, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check quickly, and then I’m still in the bathroom ages later, looking in the mirror, taking pictures from different angles, redoing my makeup or my scarf, and checking again. I’ll ask my husband if my face looks lopsided even when I know I’ve already asked.\n\nAnd then sometimes I end up looking at other women’s photos, comparing, just to see how bad mine looks next to theirs. I don’t feel proud of it. It’s not like I want to spend hours doing that… it just feels like I have to know before I can go out. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think it would be possible for you to change your opinion about the appearance of your mouth and jaw, or do you feel like your view of it is fixed?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I could change my mind about it… I really would. I think it’s more that I believe it’s actually flawed… or at least that’s how it feels to me. I keep seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw in photos and reflections, and I can’t really convince myself it’s not there.

It’s just… everything feels like it’s starting to fall apart around this one thing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of your concerns about your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I feel a bit embarrassed saying it.\n\nBefore work, or if we’re going to a family event, I can get stuck in the bathroom for a long time checking the mirror. Sometimes it’s not just once or twice… it can turn into hours, taking pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to see if my mouth looks uneven, fixing my makeup, adjusting a scarf, then checking again.\n\nI’ll ask my husband, “Does my face look lopsided?” probably more than is fair to him. And sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women, comparing my face to theirs, which I know isn’t helpful. I don’t want to sound vain or silly, but it’s hard to stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, like checking mirrors or asking your husband, or can you stop yourself when you want to?\n\nPatient: I want to say I can stop, because I know how it sounds… but honestly, once I’ve started, it feels really hard to pull away.\n\nBefore work or a family event, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check quickly, and then I’m still in the bathroom ages later, looking in the mirror, taking pictures from different angles, redoing my makeup or my scarf, and checking again. I’ll ask my husband if my face looks lopsided even when I know I’ve already asked.\n\nAnd then sometimes I end up looking at other women’s photos, comparing, just to see how bad mine looks next to theirs. I don’t feel proud of it. It’s not like I want to spend hours doing that… it just feels like I have to know before I can go out. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think it would be possible for you to change your opinion about the appearance of your mouth and jaw, or do you feel like your view of it is fixed?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I could change my mind about it… I really would. I don’t want to be someone who’s just stubborn about this.\n\nBut when I see my mouth or my jaw in a photo, or even just catch it in a reflection, the droop and the unevenness feel so obvious to me. Even if my husband or other people say I look fine, part of me thinks they’re just being kind, or they don’t see it the way I do.\n\nSo… I suppose my view feels pretty fixed right now. I’m here because I can’t seem to stop seeing it, and it’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations or seeing clients because I feel like everyone will notice. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your mouth and jaw are more about how you look to others or about something feeling 'not right' to you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly about how I look, or how I imagine I look to other people. But it also feels like something is just wrong when I see it, if that makes sense.\n\nI’m here because I can’t seem to stop noticing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. In photos or reflections, it just jumps out at me. Even when people say I look fine, I feel like it’s obvious, and then I worry others must see it too.\n\nI know that may sound a bit excessive, but it’s gotten to where I’ve avoided presentations and meeting clients because I’m afraid they’ll be looking at that instead of listening to me. Is that the sort of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance or thinking about your mouth and jaw is something you can control, or does it feel like it's happening automatically?\n\nPatient: It feels… more automatic than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself I’m just going to check once and then get on with things, but before work or if we’re going to see family, it can turn into hours in the bathroom.\n\nI’ll check the mirror, then take pictures on my phone from different angles, fix my makeup or my scarf, ask my husband if my face looks lopsided… and sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women to compare. I know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not doing it because I enjoy it. It’s like once the thought is there, I have to keep checking until I feel sure, and I don’t really feel sure for long.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific times of the day when your concerns about your mouth and jaw interfere with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… especially in the mornings, before I have to go to work or see people. I can lose so much time trying to make myself look acceptable that the day starts badly before I’ve even left the house.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it has interfered more than I’d like. I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I just couldn’t face standing there with everyone looking at me. And there have been some award-related networking events I should have gone to, but I’ve missed them because I couldn’t bear being seen.\n\nIt’s starting to affect project deadlines, and even family gatherings have become difficult. I know that’s not good… I’m trying not to let it run everything, but it feels like things are kind of falling apart around this. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you think about your mouth and jaw, are you more worried about the actual appearance or what others might think of it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the appearance itself… the droop, the unevenness around my mouth and jaw. I can’t seem to not see it in photos or reflections.\n\nBut then almost immediately I start thinking, well, if it looks that obvious to me, other people must notice it too. Even when someone tells me I look fine, I don’t really believe it for long. I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because I worry they’ll be looking at that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nI know that may sound a bit much, but… that’s honestly how it feels.\n\nDoctor: When you worry about your mouth and jaw, is it more about the flaw itself or the fear of what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: I think… it starts with the flaw itself, if that makes sense. I can’t stop seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw, especially in photos or reflections.\n\nAnd then I suppose it turns into worrying that other people must see it too. Even when they tell me I look fine, I don’t really believe it for long. I know that may sound vain or silly, but it feels so obvious to me that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because of it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you think about your mouth and jaw, is your main worry that there's something inherently wrong with how they look, or is it more that you're afraid of what other people will think of you when they see you?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I feel there’s something wrong with how it looks, I think. I can’t stop seeing the droop and the unevenness around my mouth and jaw, especially in photos or reflections.\n\nAnd then… I suppose I do worry what people think, because if it’s that obvious to me, I assume they must notice it too. Even when people say I look fine, I have a hard time believing them. I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because of it, which I know probably sounds excessive… but that’s what happens. Is that answering it properly?\n\nDoctor: When you worry about your mouth and jaw, is it because you believe they are actually flawed or because you fear others will judge you negatively?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I believe it’s actually flawed… or at least that’s how it feels to me. I keep seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw in photos and reflections, and I can’t really convince myself it’s not there.\n\nI do worry people will judge me, but that comes after—like, if it looks that obvious to me, then surely they must see it too. Even when they tell me I look fine, I don’t really believe it for very long. I’ve even started avoiding presentations and clients because of it, which I know probably sounds extreme… but that’s honestly where I’m at. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you think about your mouth and jaw, is your main concern that they look imperfect to you, or that others might view them negatively?\n\nPatient: Mostly that they look imperfect to me, I think. I mean… I keep seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw in every photo or reflection, and once I notice it, I can’t really unsee it.\n\nI do worry other people might see it negatively too, but that’s more because it feels so obvious to me. Even when people say I look fine, I try to accept it, but it doesn’t really stick. I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because of it, which I know probably sounds a bit much… but yes, the main thing is how wrong it looks to me. Is that the sort of distinction you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Is that the sort of distinction you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you worry about your mouth and jaw, is it because you are afraid of social rejection or because you genuinely believe there is something physically wrong with them?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / architect / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw in every photo and reflection, and even when people tell me I look fine, it feels so obvious that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more that I genuinely believe there’s something physically wrong with them… with how my mouth and jaw look. I keep seeing this droop, this unevenness, in photos and reflections, and it just feels so obvious to me.

I do worry people might react badly, but that’s kind of secondary, I think. I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because I can’t stop thinking they’ll notice it too. I know that may sound like I’m making too much of it, but it really does feel wrong when I see it. Is that the kind of answer you mean?

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you for seeing me. I’m probably making a bigger deal of it than I should, but I’ve been getting really stuck on how my mouth and jaw look. There’s this droop, or unevenness, that I see in photos and reflections, and I can’t seem to stop noticing it.\n\nOther people tell me I look fine, so I know this may sound a bit silly, but to me it feels very obvious. It’s gotten to the point where I’ve started avoiding presentations and meeting with clients, which is… not great for my work.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your mouth and jaw that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you also worry about?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s my mouth and jaw. I mean, I notice other things like anyone does, but this is the thing I get stuck on—the droop, the unevenness around my mouth. I see it in photos or even just reflections, and then it’s hard to unsee it.\n\nI know people say I look fine, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it feels really obvious to me… enough that I’ve been avoiding presentations and meeting clients. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about your mouth and jaw is causing you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty distressing, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s there most days, maybe every day. I’ll catch it in a photo or a reflection and then I can’t stop seeing the droop or unevenness around my mouth and jaw.\n\nEven when people tell me I look fine, it still feels really obvious to me. It’s upsetting enough that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients, which I know isn’t good. I’m not sure if that answers it the right way, but it feels like it’s taking up a lot of space in my day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family due to your concerns about your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit. I’ve been trying to keep it from affecting people around me, but it sort of is.\n\nI cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I just couldn’t face being seen like that, and I’ve been skipping some networking things connected to an award, which sounds awful when I say it out loud. Then deadlines start slipping, and at home I’ve missed or backed out of family gatherings too.\n\nI don’t mean to be difficult or dramatic. It’s just… everything feels like it’s starting to fall apart around this one thing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of your concerns about your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I feel a bit embarrassed saying it.\n\nBefore work, or if we’re going to a family event, I can get stuck in the bathroom for a long time checking the mirror. Sometimes it’s not just once or twice… it can turn into hours, taking pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to see if my mouth looks uneven, fixing my makeup, adjusting a scarf, then checking again.\n\nI’ll ask my husband, “Does my face look lopsided?” probably more than is fair to him. And sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women, comparing my face to theirs, which I know isn’t helpful. I don’t want to sound vain or silly, but it’s hard to stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, like checking mirrors or asking your husband, or can you stop yourself when you want to?\n\nPatient: I want to say I can stop, because I know how it sounds… but honestly, once I’ve started, it feels really hard to pull away.\n\nBefore work or a family event, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check quickly, and then I’m still in the bathroom ages later, looking in the mirror, taking pictures from different angles, redoing my makeup or my scarf, and checking again. I’ll ask my husband if my face looks lopsided even when I know I’ve already asked.\n\nAnd then sometimes I end up looking at other women’s photos, comparing, just to see how bad mine looks next to theirs. I don’t feel proud of it. It’s not like I want to spend hours doing that… it just feels like I have to know before I can go out. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think it would be possible for you to change your opinion about the appearance of your mouth and jaw, or do you feel like your view of it is fixed?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I could change my mind about it… I really would. I don’t want to be someone who’s just stubborn about this.\n\nBut when I see my mouth or my jaw in a photo, or even just catch it in a reflection, the droop and the unevenness feel so obvious to me. Even if my husband or other people say I look fine, part of me thinks they’re just being kind, or they don’t see it the way I do.\n\nSo… I suppose my view feels pretty fixed right now. I’m here because I can’t seem to stop seeing it, and it’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations or seeing clients because I feel like everyone will notice. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your mouth and jaw are more about how you look to others or about something feeling 'not right' to you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly about how I look, or how I imagine I look to other people. But it also feels like something is just wrong when I see it, if that makes sense.\n\nI’m here because I can’t seem to stop noticing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. In photos or reflections, it just jumps out at me. Even when people say I look fine, I feel like it’s obvious, and then I worry others must see it too.\n\nI know that may sound a bit excessive, but it’s gotten to where I’ve avoided presentations and meeting clients because I’m afraid they’ll be looking at that instead of listening to me. Is that the sort of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance or thinking about your mouth and jaw is something you can control, or does it feel like it's happening automatically?\n\nPatient: It feels… more automatic than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself I’m just going to check once and then get on with things, but before work or if we’re going to see family, it can turn into hours in the bathroom.\n\nI’ll check the mirror, then take pictures on my phone from different angles, fix my makeup or my scarf, ask my husband if my face looks lopsided… and sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women to compare. I know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not doing it because I enjoy it. It’s like once the thought is there, I have to keep checking until I feel sure, and I don’t really feel sure for long.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific times of the day when your concerns about your mouth and jaw interfere with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… especially in the mornings, before I have to go to work or see people. I can lose so much time trying to make myself look acceptable that the day starts badly before I’ve even left the house.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it has interfered more than I’d like. I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I just couldn’t face standing there with everyone looking at me. And there have been some award-related networking events I should have gone to, but I’ve missed them because I couldn’t bear being seen.\n\nIt’s starting to affect project deadlines, and even family gatherings have become difficult. I know that’s not good… I’m trying not to let it run everything, but it feels like things are kind of falling apart around this. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you think about your mouth and jaw, are you more worried about the actual appearance or what others might think of it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the appearance itself… the droop, the unevenness around my mouth and jaw. I can’t seem to not see it in photos or reflections.\n\nBut then almost immediately I start thinking, well, if it looks that obvious to me, other people must notice it too. Even when someone tells me I look fine, I don’t really believe it for long. I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because I worry they’ll be looking at that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nI know that may sound a bit much, but… that’s honestly how it feels.\n\nDoctor: When you worry about your mouth and jaw, is it more about the flaw itself or the fear of what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: I think… it starts with the flaw itself, if that makes sense. I can’t stop seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw, especially in photos or reflections.\n\nAnd then I suppose it turns into worrying that other people must see it too. Even when they tell me I look fine, I don’t really believe it for long. I know that may sound vain or silly, but it feels so obvious to me that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because of it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you think about your mouth and jaw, is your main worry that there's something inherently wrong with how they look, or is it more that you're afraid of what other people will think of you when they see you?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I feel there’s something wrong with how it looks, I think. I can’t stop seeing the droop and the unevenness around my mouth and jaw, especially in photos or reflections.\n\nAnd then… I suppose I do worry what people think, because if it’s that obvious to me, I assume they must notice it too. Even when people say I look fine, I have a hard time believing them. I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because of it, which I know probably sounds excessive… but that’s what happens. Is that answering it properly?\n\nDoctor: When you worry about your mouth and jaw, is it because you believe they are actually flawed or because you fear others will judge you negatively?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I believe it’s actually flawed… or at least that’s how it feels to me. It’s… mostly the flaw itself, I think. I can’t stop seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw, especially in photos or reflections. It feels so obvious to me, even when people tell me I look fine.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you for seeing me. I’m probably making a bigger deal of it than I should, but I’ve been getting really stuck on how my mouth and jaw look. There’s this droop, or unevenness, that I see in photos and reflections, and I can’t seem to stop noticing it.\n\nOther people tell me I look fine, so I know this may sound a bit silly, but to me it feels very obvious. It’s gotten to the point where I’ve started avoiding presentations and meeting with clients, which is… not great for my work.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your mouth and jaw that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you also worry about?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s my mouth and jaw. I mean, I notice other things like anyone does, but this is the thing I get stuck on—the droop, the unevenness around my mouth. I see it in photos or even just reflections, and then it’s hard to unsee it.\n\nI know people say I look fine, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it feels really obvious to me… enough that I’ve been avoiding presentations and meeting clients. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about your mouth and jaw is causing you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty distressing, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s there most days, maybe every day. I’ll catch it in a photo or a reflection and then I can’t stop seeing the droop or unevenness around my mouth and jaw.\n\nEven when people tell me I look fine, it still feels really obvious to me. It’s upsetting enough that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients, which I know isn’t good. I’m not sure if that answers it the right way, but it feels like it’s taking up a lot of space in my day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family due to your concerns about your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit. I’ve been trying to keep it from affecting people around me, but it sort of is.\n\nI cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I just couldn’t face being seen like that, and I’ve been skipping some networking things connected to an award, which sounds awful when I say it out loud. Then deadlines start slipping, and at home I’ve missed or backed out of family gatherings too.\n\nI don’t mean to be difficult or dramatic. It’s just… everything feels like it’s starting to fall apart around this one thing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of your concerns about your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I feel a bit embarrassed saying it.\n\nBefore work, or if we’re going to a family event, I can get stuck in the bathroom for a long time checking the mirror. Sometimes it’s not just once or twice… it can turn into hours, taking pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to see if my mouth looks uneven, fixing my makeup, adjusting a scarf, then checking again.\n\nI’ll ask my husband, “Does my face look lopsided?” probably more than is fair to him. And sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women, comparing my face to theirs, which I know isn’t helpful. I don’t want to sound vain or silly, but it’s hard to stop once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, like checking mirrors or asking your husband, or can you stop yourself when you want to?\n\nPatient: I want to say I can stop, because I know how it sounds… but honestly, once I’ve started, it feels really hard to pull away.\n\nBefore work or a family event, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check quickly, and then I’m still in the bathroom ages later, looking in the mirror, taking pictures from different angles, redoing my makeup or my scarf, and checking again. I’ll ask my husband if my face looks lopsided even when I know I’ve already asked.\n\nAnd then sometimes I end up looking at other women’s photos, comparing, just to see how bad mine looks next to theirs. I don’t feel proud of it. It’s not like I want to spend hours doing that… it just feels like I have to know before I can go out. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think it would be possible for you to change your opinion about the appearance of your mouth and jaw, or do you feel like your view of it is fixed?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I could change my mind about it… I really would. I don’t want to be someone who’s just stubborn about this.\n\nBut when I see my mouth or my jaw in a photo, or even just catch it in a reflection, the droop and the unevenness feel so obvious to me. Even if my husband or other people say I look fine, part of me thinks they’re just being kind, or they don’t see it the way I do.\n\nSo… I suppose my view feels pretty fixed right now. I’m here because I can’t seem to stop seeing it, and it’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations or seeing clients because I feel like everyone will notice. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your mouth and jaw are more about how you look to others or about something feeling 'not right' to you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly about how I look, or how I imagine I look to other people. But it also feels like something is just wrong when I see it, if that makes sense.\n\nI’m here because I can’t seem to stop noticing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. In photos or reflections, it just jumps out at me. Even when people say I look fine, I feel like it’s obvious, and then I worry others must see it too.\n\nI know that may sound a bit excessive, but it’s gotten to where I’ve avoided presentations and meeting clients because I’m afraid they’ll be looking at that instead of listening to me. Is that the sort of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance or thinking about your mouth and jaw is something you can control, or does it feel like it's happening automatically?\n\nPatient: It feels… more automatic than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself I’m just going to check once and then get on with things, but before work or if we’re going to see family, it can turn into hours in the bathroom.\n\nI’ll check the mirror, then take pictures on my phone from different angles, fix my makeup or my scarf, ask my husband if my face looks lopsided… and sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women to compare. I know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not doing it because I enjoy it. It’s like once the thought is there, I have to keep checking until I feel sure, and I don’t really feel sure for long.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific times of the day when your concerns about your mouth and jaw interfere with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… especially in the mornings, before I have to go to work or see people. I can lose so much time trying to make myself look acceptable that the day starts badly before I’ve even left the house.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it has interfered more than I’d like. I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I just couldn’t face standing there with everyone looking at me. And there have been some award-related networking events I should have gone to, but I’ve missed them because I couldn’t bear being seen.\n\nIt’s starting to affect project deadlines, and even family gatherings have become difficult. I know that’s not good… I’m trying not to let it run everything, but it feels like things are kind of falling apart around this. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you think about your mouth and jaw, are you more worried about the actual appearance or what others might think of it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the appearance itself… the droop, the unevenness around my mouth and jaw. I can’t seem to not see it in photos or reflections.\n\nBut then almost immediately I start thinking, well, if it looks that obvious to me, other people must notice it too. Even when someone tells me I look fine, I don’t really believe it for long. I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because I worry they’ll be looking at that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nI know that may sound a bit much, but… that’s honestly how it feels.\n\nDoctor: When you worry about your mouth and jaw, is it more about the flaw itself or the fear of what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: I think… it starts with the flaw itself, if that makes sense. I can’t stop seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw, especially in photos or reflections.\n\nAnd then I suppose it turns into worrying that other people must see it too. Even when they tell me I look fine, I don’t really believe it for long. I know that may sound vain or silly, but it feels so obvious to me that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because of it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you think about your mouth and jaw, is your main worry that there's something inherently wrong with how they look, or is it more that you're afraid of what other people will think of you when they see you?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I feel there’s something wrong with how it looks, I think. I can’t stop seeing the droop and the unevenness around my mouth and jaw, especially in photos or reflections.\n\nAnd then… I suppose I do worry what people think, because if it’s that obvious to me, I assume they must notice it too. Even when people say I look fine, I have a hard time believing them. I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because of it, which I know probably sounds excessive… but that’s what happens. Is that answering it properly?\n\nDoctor: When you worry about your mouth and jaw, is it because you believe they are actually flawed or because you fear others will judge you negatively?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I believe it’s actually flawed… or at least that’s how it feels to me. I keep seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw in photos and reflections, and I can’t really convince myself it’s not there.\n\nI do worry people will judge me, but that comes after—like, if it looks that obvious to me, then surely they must see it too. Even when they tell me I look fine, I don’t really believe it for very long. I’ve even started avoiding presentations and clients because of it, which I know probably sounds extreme… but that’s honestly where I’m at. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you think about your mouth and jaw, is your main concern that they look imperfect to you, or that others might view them negatively?\n\nPatient: Mostly that they look imperfect to me, I think. I mean… I keep seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw in every photo or reflection, and once I notice it, I can’t really unsee it.\n\nI do worry other people might see it negatively too, but that’s more because it feels so obvious to me. Is that the sort of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you worry about your mouth and jaw, is it because you are afraid of social rejection or because you genuinely believe there is something physically wrong with them?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I genuinely believe there’s something physically wrong with them… with how my mouth and jaw look. Is that the kind of answer you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you think about your mouth and jaw, is it the potential flaw itself that bothers you or the thought of others noticing it?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly the flaw itself, I think. But it also feels like something is just wrong when I see it, if that makes sense.\n\nI’m here because I can’t seem to stop noticing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. Is that the sort of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance or thinking about your mouth and jaw is something you can control, or does it feel like it's happening automatically?\n\nPatient: It feels… more automatic than I’d like to admit. It’s like once the thought is there, I have to keep checking until I feel sure, and I don’t really feel sure for long.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific times of the day when your concerns about your mouth and jaw interfere with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… especially in the mornings, before I have to go to work or see people.
